L34: 1 Executive Summary
L35: 1.1. Product Introduction
L36: Glecaprevir (GLE) and pibrentasvir (PIB) is a fixed-dosed combination (FDC) tablet that contains two new molecular entities (NMEs). GLE is a hepatitis C virus (HCV) NS3/4A protease inhibitor (PI) and PIB is an HCV NS5A inhibitor, neither of which is available as a single drug. The proposed indication is for treatment of chronic HCV genotype (GT) 1 through 6 infection in adults, including those with severe renal disease, HIV-co-infection and a subpopulation of those with GT1 infection with previous HCV directly acting antiviral therapy. The recommended dosage is three (100 mg/40 mg) tablets taken orally with food once daily. The originally proposed treatment duration is dependent upon prior treatment experience and GT.
L37: The original proposed dosage regimens and durations for HCV mono-infected and HCV/HIV-1 co-infected patients including those with severe renal disease were as follows:
L38: Treatment-Naïve:
L39: • GT1-6 without cirrhosis: 8 weeks• GT1-6 with compensated (Childs Turcotte Pugh A) cirrhosis: 12 weeks
L40: Treatment-Experienced:
L41: A large rectangular gray box redacting the content for the 'Treatment-Experienced' section. In the top right corner of the redacted area, the text '(b) (4)' is visible.Redacted content
L42: NOTE: BOC = boceprevir; GLE = glecaprevir; GT = genotype; NS5A = nonstructural protein 5A; PIB = pibrentasvir; P/R = regimens containing interferon, pegylated interferon, and/or ribavirin; RBV = ribavirin; SMV = simprevir; SOF = sofosbuvir; TE = treatment-experienced; TVR = telaprevir
L43: 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L44: The Application contains substantial evidence of effectiveness required by law 21 CFR 314.126(a)(b) to support approval of GLE/PIB for treatment of chronic HCV GT 1 to 6 infection in adults with and without compensated Child-Turcotte-Pugh (CTP) A cirrhosis, including in those with severe renal impairment and HCV/HIV-1 co-infection. In addition, the Application contains substantial evidence of effectiveness to support an indication for patients with HCV GT1 infection and prior treatment experience with NS5A inhibitors or NS3/4A protease inhibitors (but not both).
L45: Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials evaluated GLE/PIB in various subpopulations, including GT 1- 6 infected subjects, cirrhotics and non-cirrhotics, HCV/HIV-1 coinfected subjects, those with severe renal impairment, and treatment naïve (TN) and treatment experienced (TE) subjects. The overall sustained virologic response (SVR12) rates, considered a virologic cure, across trials for the durations recommended by the FDA review team were 91-100%. The number of GT 1- 4 infected subjects studied and the effectiveness of GLE/PIB for these genotypes was adequate for the recommended durations of 8 weeks for patients without cirrhosis and 12 weeks for those with compensated (CTP A) cirrhosis. The number of genotype 5 and 6 subjects and efficacy in this subpopulation was sufficient to support an indication for 12 weeks for patients without and with compensated CTP A cirrhosis. In addition, the NDA contained sufficient data to support an indication for patients with GT1 infection and treatment experience with NS5A inhibitors or NS3/4A protease inhibitors.
L46: However, the data were too limited to support approval of GLE/PIB for 8 weeks duration for treatment-naïve subjects without cirrhosis with HCV GT 5 (n=2) or GT 6 (n=9) infection. However, there were adequate data to support approval of a 12 week duration for treatment naïve HCV GT 5 (n=22) and GT 6 (n=28) patients without cirrhosis; the overall SVR12 rate was 100% for both genotypes. In order to shorten the duration from 12 weeks to 8 weeks in labeling, the Applicant must provide data on an adequate number of treatment-naïve HCV GT 5 or 6 subjects without cirrhosis who are successfully treated with GLE/PIB for 8 weeks duration.
L47: The NDA also does not contain substantial evidence of effectiveness
L48: (b) (4)
L49: given the lack of data submitted for other genotypes. Additionally, the NDA does not contain substantial evidence of effectiveness to support an indication for HCV GT1 patients with both NS5A inhibitor and NS3/4A protease inhibitor treatment experience.
L50: 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L51: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mayret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L52: Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L53: Glecaprevir (GLE) is a hepatitis C virus (HCV) NS3/4A protease inhibitor and pibrentasvir (PIB) is an HCV NS5A inhibitor. GLE/PIB is a fixed-dose combination tablet with a proposed indication for treatment of chronic HCV genotypes (GTs) 1 through 6 infection in adults. Intended subpopulations include treatment-naïve (TN) and treatment-experienced (TE) patients including a subpopulation of those with previous exposure to directly acting antiviral therapy (DAAs), patients with advanced chronic kidney disease (CKD), including those receiving hemodialysis and patients with HIV-co-infection.
L54: HCV infection is a serious disease, affecting an estimated 3 million people in the U.S. and over 100 million people worldwide. Although often asymptomatic in early stages, if untreated, chronic HCV can lead to debilitating and life-threatening liver problems, including hepatocellular carcinoma, liver failure, and death. The current standard of care treatments for HCV GT 1-6 infection consist of oral direct-acting antivirals (DAAs) that result in sustained virologic response determined 12 weeks after the end of treatment (SVR12), considered a virologic cure, in up to 93-99% of patients. However, a major treatment gap remains for CKD receiving dialysis in patients infected with GTs 2, 3, 5 and 6 as no IFN-sparing regimens are currently available for this patient population. Additionally, treatment options are needed for patients who have failed DAA-only treatment, such as NS5A inhibitor-containing regimens or NS3/4A inhibitor-containing regimens.
L55: GLE/PIB demonstrated SVR12 rates ranging from 91-100% for treatment durations recommended by the FDA review team. SVR rates varied depending on the regimen, patients' HCV GT, and patients' prior treatment history. Efficacy was similar in patients with or without cirrhosis, with or without HIV coinfection, and with CKD, with or without hemodialysis. GLE/PIB represents the first DAA therapy recommended for a duration of as little as 8 weeks for HCV GT 1 -4 treatment-naïve patients without cirrhosis and the first IFN-free regimen for treatment of HCV GTs 2, 3, 5 and 6 in severe CKD patients, including those receiving hemodialysis. GLE/PIB is also an effective option for HCV GT 1 infected patients with prior DAA treatment experience with NS5A inhibitors or NS3/4A protease inhibitors. Because of a high virologic failure rate, the data do not support the use of GLE/PIB in patients with prior treatment experience with both NS5A inhibitors and NS3/4A protease inhibitors. Additionally, there were too few treatment-naïve, non-cirrhotic subjects with GT 5 or 6 infection evaluated for an 8-week duration, although there were sufficient data to recommend a 12 week duration for both cirrhotic and non-cirrhotic subjects with GT 5 or 6 infection.
L56: No major safety issues specifically related to GLE/PIB were identified in this review. Headache (9%), fatigue (8%), nausea (6%), and diarrhea (5%) were the most common ADRs and occurred at greater than or equal to 5% in ENDURANCE-2, the 12 week placebo controlled study of subjects without severe renal impairment. All ADRs, except fatigue and asthenia, occurred at higher rates in the GLE/PIB arm of the placebo
L57: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L58: controlled trial. They were also reported in fairly similar proportions across major clinical trials, in which subjects without severe renal impairment received GLE/PIB. Most ADRs were grade 1 or 2 and mild or moderate in severity across all Phase 2 and 3 clinical trials. In subjects without severe renal impairment, there only one serious adverse event, transient ischemic attack, considered by the Investigator as reasonably related to the study drug. The proportion of subjects who permanently discontinued treatment due to adverse reactions was 0.3% (7/2,265). In subjects with severe renal impairment, ADRs observed in greater than or equal to 5% of subjects receiving 12 weeks of treatment with GLE/PIB were pruritus (17%), fatigue (12%), nausea (9%), asthenia (7%), and headache (6%). In subjects treated with GLE/PIB who reported an adverse reaction, 90% had adverse reactions of mild or moderate severity (Grade 1 or 2). No subjects experienced a serious adverse reaction. The proportion of subjects who permanently discontinued treatment due to adverse reactions was 2%.
L59: The safety in EXPEDITION-4, which evaluated GLE/PIB for 12 weeks duration in subjects with chronic Stage 4 and Stage 5 renal disease, including those on hemodialysis, was similar to the overall population. The most common adverse reactions observed in greater than or equal to 5% of subjects were pruritus (17%), fatigue (12%), nausea (9%), asthenia (7%), and headache (6%). In subjects treated with GLE/PIB who reported an adverse reaction, 90% had adverse reactions of mild or moderate severity (Grade 1 or 2). The proportion of subjects who permanently discontinued treatment due to adverse reactions was 2%.
L60: Approval of GLE/PIB for treatment of adult patients with HCV GT 1 through 6 infection is fully supported by the available evidence of efficacy and safety. The Phase 2 and 3 Efficacy Data support the FDA indicated GLE/PIB treatment regimens in the following patient populations, some of which differ from the original Applicant proposal :
L61: Treatment-naïve
L62: GTs 1, 2, 3, or 4 subjects without cirrhosis for 8 weeks
L63: GTs 1, 2, 3, or 4 with compensated Cirrhosis (ChildPugh A) for 12 weeks
L64: GT5 or 6 with or without Compensated cirrhosis 12 weeks
L65: Treatment-experience-PRS (defined as prior treatment experience with regimens containing interferon, pegylated interferon, ribavirin and/or sofosbuvir, but no prior treatment experience with an NS5A inhibitor or NS3/4A protease inhibitor)
L66: GTs 1, 2 or 4 without cirrhosis for 8 weeks
L67: GT1, 2 or 4 with compensated cirrhosis for 12 weeks

L69: GT3 with or without cirrhosis for 16 weeks
L70: GT5 or 6 with or without compensated cirrhosis 12 weeks
L71: Treatment-experienced with a regimen containing an HCV NS5A Inhibitor or NS3/4A Inhibitor (Not Both)
L72: GT1 with or without compensated cirrhosis and any prior treatment regimen containing an NS5A inhibitor (no prior NS3/4A protease inhibitor) for 16 weeks
L73: GT1 with or without compensated cirrhosis and any prior treatment regimen containing an NS3/4A protease inhibitor (no prior NS5A inhibitor) for 12 weeks
L74: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons




Analysis of Condition


• Chronic hepatitis C viral infection (HCV infection) causes inflammation of the liver that can lead to long-term health problems or death.
• Globally it is estimated that over 100 million people are infected with HCV, including approximately 3 million people in the United States (U.S.).
• The prevalence rate of HCV among patients undergoing hemodialysis has been reported as 7.8%, and it is estimated that over 60,000 HCV-infected patients will require HD by 2020.
• There are at least six distinct HCV genotypes (GTs). Most common among U.S. patients is GT 1 (72%), followed by GT 2 (11%), GT 3 (9%), and GT 4 (6%). GTs 5 and 6 occur uncommonly (\leq 1\%) in the U.S. but may predominate in other parts of the world.
• HCV infection is typically asymptomatic in its early stages. However, if left untreated, HCV infection can lead to cirrhosis, hepatocellular carcinoma, liver failure, and death. HCV infection is a leading cause of chronic liver disease in the U.S.


If untreated, chronic HCV infection is a life-threatening condition, one that affects a large population. Patients can experience symptoms that are severe and debilitating. HCV infection is a significant and growing public health concern.
L75: Clinical Review
  Larissa Stabinski, MD
  Aimee Hodowanec, MD
  NDA 209394
  Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L76: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons




Current Treatment Options


The current standard-of-care treatments for CHC are interferon-free, all-oral DAA regimens. Treatment options vary based on HCV GT, however all currently approved regimens, have a 12 week or longer indication (with the exception of ledipasvir/sofosbuvir for which 8 weeks can be considered in GT1 treatment-naïve patients without cirrhosis who have pretreatment HCV RNA &lt;6 million IU/mL ):
GT1: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; ledipasvir/sofosbuvir; elbasvir/grazoprevir; paritaprevir/ombitasvir/ritonavir + dasabuvir; daclatasvir (in combination with sofosbuvir); and simeprevir (in combination with sofosbuvir)
GT2: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; sofosbuvir + ribavirin
GT3: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; daclatasvir + sofosbuvir; sofosbuvir + ribavirin
GT4: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; ledipasvir/sofosbuvir; elbasvir/grazoprevir; ombitasvir/paritaprevir/ritonavir with RBV
GT5: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; ledipasvir/sofosbuvir
GT6: sofosbuvir/velpatasvir; ledipasvir/sofosbuvir
Treatment with DAAs can result in sustained virologic response determined 12 weeks after the end of treatment (SVR12), considered a virologic cure, in &gt; 93% of CHC patients with compensated liver disease and sofosbuvir/velpatasvir provides a treatment option for patients with decompensated cirrhosis. However, there remains a critical gap of treatment options for patients with severe renal impairment for GTs 2, 3, 5 and 6 and for patients with treatment experience with DAA therapy.


Patients with chronic HCV infection would greatly benefit from new therapeutic options that are well tolerated, of a shorter duration, ribavirin-free, and equally or more efficacious than current interferon-free DAA options in patients with or without severe renal impairment.
A specific unmet medical need exists for patients who have previously failed a prior DAA regimen, particularly for those who are NS5A inhibitor experienced, because currently no approved regimens are available.
L77: Clinical Review
  Larissa Stabinski, MD
  Aimee Hodowanec, MD
  NDA 209394
  Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L78: Dimension
Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons





Benefit



The efficacy of GLE/PIB was established in nine Phase 2 and 3 clinical trials which cumulatively evaluated 2,369 subjects who received GLE/PIB for 8, 12 or 16 weeks duration. The trial populations varied based on HCV GT, cirrhosis status, treatment experience and renal disease.
The primary efficacy endpoint was SVR12, or virologic cure. As displayed in the table below, SVR12 results overall, as recommended by FDA, ranged from 91-100% depending on the Phase 2 or 3 trial regimen, HCV GT, cirrhosis status, prior treatment history, and treatment duration. Efficacy did not vary according to renal or HCV/HIV-1 coinfection status.

Risk



The safety database for GLE/PIB includes 2,265 subjects without severe renal impairment and 104 subjects with renal impairment (evaluated separately for safety).
ENDURANCE-2 included a placebo-controlled comparison for safety with deferred treatment in subjects who were randomized to placebo.
No major safety issues were encountered during this review.
Headache, fatigue, nausea and diarrhea were the most commonly (&gt;=5%) reported adverse drug reactions reported across trials, excluding subjects with severe renal impairment enrolled in EXPEDITION-4. Pruritus, fatigue, nausea, asthenia and headache were the most commonly experienced adverse drug reactions in the population of subjects with severe renal impairment.


GLE/PIB demonstrated an overall favorable safety profile.



Risk Management



Although no safety signals were detected in this review, the GLE/PIB prescribing information will contain safety information regarding the risk of HBV reactivation required for all HCV DAA labels.


Safety concerns associated with GLE and PIB are adequately addressed in product labelling.
L81: 2 Therapeutic Context
L82: Analysis of Condition
L83: Chronic infection with hepatitis C virus (HCV) is a global health problem affecting an estimated 130-150 million people worldwide, including approximately 3 to 5 million people in the United States (US). At least seven different HCV GTs have been identified, numbered 1 to 7, with further breakdown into subtypes for several of the known GTs (e.g., GT 1 subtypes 1a and 1b).[1] In the US, GT 1 is the most common (70-75%; mostly subtype 1a), followed by GT 2 (11%), GT 3 (9%), and GT 4 (6%). GTs 5 and 6 occur uncommonly (\leq 1\%) in the US but may predominate in other parts of the world.[1-3]
L84: HCV is a leading cause of chronic liver disease and is currently the most common reason for liver transplantation in the US. The natural history of chronic HCV (CHC) typically involves an asymptomatic period in the early stages with progression to cirrhosis, hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC), liver failure, or death, if left untreated. Once cirrhosis is established, complications such as jaundice, ascites, variceal hemorrhage, and encephalopathy may develop which defines decompensated cirrhosis, or end-stage liver disease. In patients with decompensated cirrhosis, the 5-year survival rate is approximately 50%.[4, 5]
L85: The ultimate goal of HCV treatment is to reduce the occurrence of end-stage liver disease and its related complications by achieving a sustained virologic response (SVR), typically defined as unquantifiable HCV RNA 12 weeks following the completion of treatment (SVR12). SVR12 is generally considered a virologic cure. Achieving sustained HCV viral eradication through successful HCV treatment is associated with improvements in clinical outcomes such as decreased development of HCC, hepatic events, fibrosis, and all-cause mortality.[6-8]
L86: Over the past five years, numerous direct acting antiviral (DAA) agents have been approved for the treatment of chronic HCV infection, initially in combination with pegylated interferon and ribavirin (PR). Treatment with these early regimens resulted in substantially higher SVR rates than PR alone, but the toxicity of PR, particularly the interferon component, made these regimens suboptimal. More recently approved all-oral DAA regimens have demonstrated high SVR12 rates without use of interferon, and in many cases, without ribavirin (RBV).
L87: However, the approved regimens are not equally effective across all HCV genotypes and subpopulations. Limitations of some current regimens include the requirement of ribavirin (RBV) for certain populations, significant drug to drug interactions, limited options for subjects with renal insufficiency, reduced efficacy in patients with baseline amino acid polymorphisms
L88: associated with reduced susceptibility to the HCV nonstructural viral protein 5A (NS5A) inhibitors or nonstructural viral protein 3/4A (NS3/4A) protease inhibitors (PIs), and limited options for patients who have failed DAA- IFN-free treatment regimens. Historically, efficacy in GT3-infected patients, particularly those who are treatment-experienced (TE) and/or cirrhotic, has been less than optimal and substantially less than what is observed for other genotypes.
L89: In the current NDA, the Applicant seeks approval of GLE/PIB for the treatment of HCV GT 1-6 in subjects with or without compensated cirrhosis including those with severe renal insufficiency and prior treatment with DAA or INF-based regimens.
L90: 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L91: Treatment with interferon (IFN)-sparing DAA regimens is the current standard-of-care for all HCV GTs. Table 1 provides a brief synopsis of single agents and fixed-dose combination products that are approved for use without IFN. For additional details regarding the specific populations that are indicated (e.g. patients with cirrhosis or undergoing liver transplant), please refer to the complete prescribing information for the product of interest.

L97: 3 Regulatory Background
L98: 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L99: GLE/PIB contains two NMEs and neither component is currently marketed in the U.S.
L100: 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L101: AbbVie Inc. submitted an initial IND application for GLE (IND 116169) and for PIB (IND 116170) for treatment of chronic HCV infection on October 15, 2012. Development of the combination of GLE and PIB occurred under IND 127416. This section describes key events or activities that occurred under IND 127416 during the GLE and PIB clinical development program prior to submission of the NDA.
L102: Fast Track Designation
L103: On January 24, 2013, FDA granted AbbVie's request for Fast Track designation for the development program of GLE (IND 116169) and PIB (IND116170) in combination with other Direct Acting Antiviral Agents (DAAs), for the treatment of chronic hepatitis C virus infection.
L104: Breakthrough Therapy Designation
L105: On April 21, 2016, FDA granted AbbVie's request for Breakthrough Therapy designation for treatment of chronic HCV infection in the GT1 DAA-experienced patient population due to the unmet medical need in this patient population. As such, this application was given a Priority Review.
L106: End-of-Phase 2 Meeting
L107: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L108: FDA and the Applicant agreed upon the adequacy of the clinical pharmacology data to transition to Phase 3; Phase 3 virology approach, dose and duration selection, overall design of Phase 3 studies. FDA indicated the adequacy of proposed size and design of safety database to the support the New Drug Application. FDA reinforced the desire to have several populations, including HIV coinfected subjects included in the clinical trial population. Preliminary comments provided to the Applicant were sufficient, and the Applicant cancelled the meeting.
L109: Pre-NDA Meeting
L110: On November 3, 2016 FDA and Applicant met to gain alignment on the adequacy of the clinical data to support an NDA for the combination regimen of glecaprevir (ABT-493, GLE) and pibrentasvir (ABT-530, PIB). FDA and the Applicant had preliminary discussions on proposed dosing recommendations, including treatment durations for the studied populations; the FDA indicated that treatment durations for the different genotypes and subpopulations remained a review issue.
L111: During the pre-NDA meeting, FDA recommended the Applicant form an external expert hepatic panel to evaluate a subset of 10 subjects who had total bilirubin (tbili) \geq 2\times\text{ULN} and direct/tbili ratio &gt; 0.4 (7 subjects); had ALT &gt; 3\times\text{ULN} and tbili \geq 2\times\text{ULN} (2 subjects); had ALT increase &gt; 5\times\text{ULN} (3 subjects); met the hepatic decompensation/hepatic failure pre-defined criteria (1 subject), and provide the assessment to the Division very early in the review cycle, and later in the review cycle to provide an assessment of all subjects with a tbili \geq 2\times\text{ULN}. The Applicant agreed to these recommendations.

L114: 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety

L116: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L117: 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L118: Inspection sites were selected from four of the nine Phase 2 and 3 clinical trials submitted for review for this NDA: EXPEDITION-1 and -4, and ENDURANCE-1 and -3. A total of eight sites were selected, because both GLE and PIB are NMEs, and a substantial amount of clinical trial experience with these drugs has been at foreign sites. Multinational trials were necessary because the prevalence of HCV GTs varies between geographic regions, and including trial sites around the world enables greater accrual of subjects with HCV GTs that are uncommon in the US. It is desirable to include foreign sites in the OSI inspections to verify the quality of conduct of the trials.
L119: Six of the clinical sites, including two foreign sites, had no regulatory findings. The remaining two sites had Voluntary Action Indicated classifications, one of which is final and the other is pending. The overall conclusion of the OSI inspectional findings from the eight clinical trial sites support the validity and acceptability of the data as reported by the sponsor under this NDA.
L120: 4.2. Product Quality
L121: The commercial GLE/PIB drug product is an immediate release fixed-dose combination bilayer tablet containing 100 mg of glecaprevir in one layer and 40 mg of pibrentasvir in the other layer for once daily oral administration. The proposed dosing regimen consists of a total daily dose of glecaprevir/pibrentasvir 300 mg/120 mg (or three 100 mg/40 mg tablets daily).
L122: The tablets are pink, oblong-shaped, film-coated, and debossed with “NXT” on one side. The tablets include the following inactive ingredients: copovidone (type K 28), vitamin E (tocopherol) polyethylene glycol succinate, colloidal silicon dioxide, propylene glycol monocaprylate (type II), croscarmellose sodium, sodium stearyl fumarate, hypromellose 2910, lactose monohydrate, titanium dioxide, polyethylene glycol 3350 and iron oxide red. The tablets do not contain gluten. The approximate dimensions of each tablet are: length 18.8 mm, width 10.0 mm, and thickness 6.8 mm.
L123: The GLE/PIB tablets will be dispensed in a 4-week (monthly) or 8-week carton. Each weekly carton contains seven daily dose wallets. Each child resistant daily dose wallet contains three 100 mg/40 mg glecaprevir/pibrentasvir tablets. The products should be stored at or below 30°C (86°F).
L124: The film-coated GLE/PIB 100 mg/40 mg co-formulated tablet (Phase 3 formulation) is the proposed commercial formulation and was used in all Phase 3 studies and in select Phase 1 studies. In addition to the Phase 3 formulation, GLE and PIB were administered as individually formulated first-in-human (FIH), Phase 2a (Ph2a), and Phase 2b (Ph2b) tablets in various Phase

L127: 1 and 2 studies. Several other experimental formulations of individually- or co-formulated GLE and PIB were evaluated in bioavailability studies only.
L128: Please refer to the Office of Product Quality (OPQ) Reviews by Dr. Danuta Gromek-Woods, Dr. Balajee Shanmugam, Dr. Raymond Frankewich, Dr. Kasturi Srinivasachar, Dr. Ying Wang and Dr. Upinder Atwal for further details on manufacturing processes, process controls, formulation specifications, and the adequacy of data provided to assure drug stability, strength, purity, and quality for GLE/PIB. The final report from the inspection of the production facilities was not available at the time this review was finalized.
L129: 4.3. Clinical Microbiology
L130: This section provides a brief summary of key glecaprevir and pibrentasvir nonclinical virology characteristics to support clinical trials evaluating this combination regimen. Specific discussions of clinical virology assessments conducted during the pivotal trials, development of resistance, impact of baseline NS5A resistance associated polymorphisms, and consequences of virologic failure, are provided in Sections 6 and 7 (clinical efficacy).
L131: Hepatitis C virus is a small, positive-strand RNA virus belonging to the Flaviviridae family. At least seven HCV genotypes (GT) have been identified, numbered GT 1 to 7, with further breakdown into subtypes for several of the known GTs (e.g. GT1 subtypes 1a and 1b). DAAs act by inhibiting viral proteins involved in RNA replication. Despite having similar targets, various DAAs of the same class (e.g. NS3/4 PIs, NS5A inhibitors), may have differential degrees of activity across the various HCV GTs.
L132: Mechanism of Action
L133: GLE is an inhibitor of the HCV NS3/4A protease, which mediates cleavage of the HCV encoded polyprotein and is essential for viral replication.
L134: PIB is an inhibitor of HCV NS5A protein, which is a viral phosphoprotein essential for HCV replication.
L135: Antiviral Activity in Biochemical Assays and Cell Culture
L136: Glecaprevir
L137: In biochemical assays, GLE inhibited the activity of recombinant NS3/4A protease enzymes from HCV genotype/subtypes 1a, 1b, 2a, 2b, 3a, 4a, 5a and 6a, with IC50 values of 3.5-11.3 nM. In cell culture assays, GLE inhibited the replication of HCV replicons carrying NS3 protease genes from HCV subtypes 1a, 1b, 2a, 2b, 3a, 4a and 6a, with EC50 values of 0.85-4.6 nM, and a therapeutic index of approximately 85,000. GLE also had consistent, sub- to low nanomolar activity against a panel of transient HCV replicons derived from clinical isolates.
L138: CDER Clinical Review Template 2015 Edition
L139: 25
L140: Version date: November 5, 2015 for initial rollout (NME/original BLA reviews)
L141: In cell culture resistance selection studies, GLE exposure of HCV replicons from HCV subtypes 1a, 1b, 3a, 4a or 6a resulted in the emergence of amino acid substitutions in NS3. The most common substitutions observed occurred at NS3 amino acid position A156, which is a known NS3/4A PI resistance-associated position. The selection of amino acid substitutions at this position, in addition to others (e.g. Y56 and D/Q168) indicates that GLE has at least partially overlapping resistance mechanisms with other HCV NS3/4A PIs.
L142: Analyses of HCV replicons demonstrated that GLE maintained activity against replicons harboring individual amino acid substitutions at certain NS3 positions commonly associated with resistance to other NS3/4A PIs, including positions V36, Y56, Q80 and R155. However, GLE activity was reduced by amino acid substitutions at NS3 positions A156 and D168 (or Q168 for GT3), in some cases by as much as &gt;1,000-fold, consistent with the emergence of substitutions at these positions in GLE-selected replicons. Certain combinations of amino acid substitutions, including these key positions, further reduced GLE anti-HCV activity.
L143: Pibrentasvir
L144: PIB inhibited the replication of HCV replicons carrying NS5A genes from genotype/subtypes 1a, 1b, 2a, 2b, 3a, 4a, 5a and 6a, with EC50 values of 1.4-5 pM, and a therapeutic index &gt;10,000,000. PIB was also shown to have consistent, sub- to low pM activity against a panel of transient HCV replicons derived from clinical isolates. In cell culture combination studies, PIB had non-antagonistic antiviral activity with other anti-HCV agents including GLE (NS3/4A PI), SOF (uridine nucleotide analogue NS5B polymerase inhibitor), and RBV.
L145: In cell culture resistance selection studies, PIB exposure of HCV GT1a or GT1b replicons resulted in the emergence of amino acid substitutions in NS5A of GT1a replicons, but no PIB-resistant GT1b replicons were selected. PIB-resistant GT1a replicons primarily carried an NS5A Y93H substitution, while a few clones had emergent NS5A Q30D, Q30-deletion, Y93N, or H58D + Y93H substitutions. All of these substitutions occurred at known NS5A inhibitor resistance-associated positions, confirming that PIB is an NS5A inhibitor, and also indicating that PIB has at least partially overlapping resistance mechanisms with other NS5A inhibitors. Similar PIB resistance selection and characterization studies were conducted for replicons harboring NS5A genes from non-GT1 isolates, which again showed resistance selection at known resistance-associated positions, although, in general, few drug-resistant clones were obtained.
L146: Analyses of HCV replicons engineered to carry specific NS5A amino acid substitutions demonstrated that PIB maintained activity against replicons harboring various single amino acid substitutions associated with resistance to NS5A inhibitors. Among all of the site-directed mutant replicons evaluated across all HCV GTs, the only single NS5A amino acid substitutions that reduced PIB activity \geq 10-fold were M28G and Q30D in GT1a, and P29-deletion and P32-deletion in GT1b. Nevertheless, with the exception of the P32-deletion PIB remained active at
L147: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L148: picomolar levels against these replicons. The presence of two or more NS5A inhibitor resistance-associated substitutions had a greater impact on PIB activity, indicating that combinations of substitutions can act synergistically to confer resistance to PIB.
L149: Please refer to Dr. Patrick Harrington's Clinical Virology review for additional details.
L150: 4.4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L151: This section provides a brief review of key outcomes of the pharmacology/toxicology program. Please see the primary nonclinical review by Ilona Bebenek for complete details.
L152: Glecaprevir
L153: The oral bioavailability of GLE was 90% in the rat, 44% in the dog and 25% in the monkey. In vitro protein binding was &gt;99% in all species and &gt;97.5% in human. In radiolabeled testing, GLE was widely distributed with the highest concentrations being found in the liver in the rat. GLE does not readily cross the blood:brain barrier, blood:eye barrier, blood:testis barrier or bind to melanin. In a clinical single dose study of GLE 800 mg once daily for 10 days in healthy volunteers (M13-356), the metabolite profile of the pooled plasma determined that GLE was the major component; there were no significant circulating clinical metabolites (e.g., &gt;10% of total drug-related exposure) requiring non-clinical safety assessment. Elimination primarily occurred through the biliary route in rats and humans. GLE was transferred to rat pups during lactation.
L154: GLE was not associated with clinically relevant adverse effects on cardiovascular, neurological, or respiratory endpoints evaluated in safety pharmacology studies. No clinically relevant adverse effects were observed in pivotal repeat-dose general toxicology studies; clinically relevant non-adverse effects included elevated ALT and GGT levels in dog. GLE was not considered genotoxic based on negative results in the in vitro bacterial mutation assay, in vitro mammalian chromosome aberration assay, and in vivo rat micronucleus assay. Because GLE was not genotoxic and will be administered to humans for, at longest, a 16 week duration, carcinogenicity studies were not required. There were no clinically relevant adverse effects on male or female fertility, embryofetal development, or pre/post-natal development in rats.
L155: Pibrentasvir
L156: The oral bioavailability of GLE was 7.9% in the rat, 30% in the dog and 14% in the monkey. In vitro protein binding was &gt;99% in all species and in humans. In radiolabeled testing, PIB was widely distributed with the highest concentrations being found in the liver, kidney, fat, and large intestine in the rat. PIB does not readily cross the blood:brain barrier, blood:eye barrier, blood:testis barrier or bind to melanin. In a clinical single dose study of PIB 600 mg once daily
L157: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L158: for 10 days in healthy volunteers, the metabolite profile of the pooled plasma determined that PIB was the major component, accounting for 98.% of total drug related materials; there were no significant circulating clinical metabolites requiring non-clinical safety assessment. Elimination primarily occurred through the biliary route in rats, dogs and humans. PIB was transferred to rat pups during lactation.
L159: PIB was not associated with clinically relevant adverse effects on cardiovascular, neurological, or respiratory endpoints evaluated in safety pharmacology studies. No clinically relevant adverse effects were observed in pivotal repeat-dose general toxicology studies. PIB was not considered genotoxic based on negative results in the in vitro bacterial mutation assay, in vitro mammalian chromosome aberration assay, and in vivo rat micronucleus assay. Because PIB was not genotoxic and will be administered to humans for, at longest, a 16 week duration, carcinogenicity studies were not required. There were no clinically relevant adverse effects on male or female fertility in mice, embryofetal development in mice or rabbits, or pre/post-natal development in mice.
L160: Because there were no concerning safety signals or overlapping toxicity with GLE and PIB, a combination study was not conducted. (b) (4)
L161: a four-week safety study at low doses of GLE and PIB was conducted in Sprague-Dawley rats. There were no adverse findings in this rat safety study. The AUC exposures for GLE were equivalent to or 2-fold higher than exposures at the recommended clinical dose in cirrhotic and non-cirrhotic patients, respectively.
L162: 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L163: 4.5.1. Mechanism of Action
L164: GLE is a nonstructural (NS) protein 3/NS protein 4A (NS3/4A) protease inhibitor of hepatitis C virus (HCV). PIB is an NS protein 5A (NS5A) inhibitor.
L165: 4.5.2. Pharmacodynamics
L166: The final selection of the GLE and PIB doses was informed by the results of the monotherapy study M13-595. The results demonstrated that a GLE dose \geq 100 mg QD or a PIB dose \geq 40 mg QD achieved an approximately 4 log10 viral load decline with 3 days of therapy among GT1-infected subjects. GLE exposures at a 300 mg dose and PIB exposures at 120 mg were expected to provide near maximal antiviral effect, and higher doses would not result in further meaningful reductions in HCV RNA.
L167: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L168: The proposed dosing regimen of 300 mg GLE/120 mg PIB QD is acceptable in the general population because high SVR12 (&gt; 90%) rates were observed across HCV genotype 1-6 in both treatment naïve and treatment-experienced patients in the phase 2 and 3 trials. Exposure-response efficacy analyses based on the Phase 2 and 3 trials results could not identify a role of both GLE and PIB exposures on outcome, so assessments were instead based on Phase 2 dose-ranging results. Limited data from lower doses of GLE and PIB support that a decrease in response may be anticipated in more difficult to treat genotypes, such as genotype 3, with a 50% decrease in exposures. Exposure-response safety analyses based on the same data support the safety of 300 mg GLE/120 mg PIB in HCV-infected patients.
L169: 4.5.3. Pharmacokinetics
L170: Adsorption
L171: When co-administered with PIB under fed conditions, the Tmax of GLE occurs 3 to 5 hours after dosing. GLE is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP transporters. When co-administered with GLE under fed conditions, the Tmax of PIB occurs approximately 5 hours after dosing. PIB is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP transporters. Mean GLE and PIB exposures increased by 163% and 40 %, respectively, with moderate fat meals, and by 114% and 105%, respectively, with high fat meals.
L172: Distribution
L173: GLE is approximately 97.5% bound to human plasma proteins, independent of concentration from 0.1 to 30 µM (800 to 25,200 ng/mL). Mean human blood-to-plasma ratio of GLE is 0.57. The steady state apparent volume of distribution is 170 L.
L174: PIB is greater than 99.9% bound to human plasma proteins, independent of concentration from 0.1 to 30 µM (110 to 33400 ng/mL). Mean human blood-to-plasma concentration ratio of PIB is 0.62. The steady state apparent volume of distribution is 3630 L.
L175: Metabolism
L176: GLE exhibited limited metabolism in vitro, predominantly by CYP3A4/5, and to a much less extent by CYP2D6, CYP2C9, and CYP2C8. Metabolism plays a minimal role in the elimination of PIB.
L177: Elimination
L178: Both GLE and PIB are predominantly excreted through the biliary-fecal route with 92.1 % and 96.6 % of the administered dose recovered in the feces, respectively. The terminal elimination half-life of GLE and PIB is 6 hours and 13 hours, respectively.
L179: Intrinsic Factors
L180: Hepatic Impairment: A dedicated hepatic impairment study demonstrated that, relative to healthy subjects, GLE AUC is 11-fold higher in subjects with Child-Pugh C cirrhosis. GLE AUC was also numerically higher (AUC ratio= 2.0) in subjects with Child-Pugh B cirrhosis. PIB AUC did not change in subjects with Child-Pugh B cirrhosis and was 2-fold higher in subjects with Child-Pugh C cirrhosis. Based on these data, the regimen is contraindicated in subjects with Child-Pugh C cirrhosis. Currently there are no clinical data available for GLE/PIB in subjects with Child-Pugh B or C decompensated cirrhosis. Efficacy and safety of GLE/PIB in HCV infected subjects with Child-Pugh B decompensated cirrhosis should be established in clinical efficacy and safety trials in order to extend dosing recommendations to subjects with Child-Pugh B cirrhosis.
L181: Renal Impairment: In a dedicated renal impairment study, the highest AUC change was an increase by 45% and 46% of GLE and PIB, respectively. GLE and PIB are minimally eliminated via the renal route (&lt;0.7% of dose); thus, no dosage adjustments are proposed in patients with mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment or patients with end-stage renal disease with or without hemodialysis. Both drugs are not removed by dialysis due to high protein binding. It should be noted that the applicant conducted a single-arm, open label trial (M15-462) to evaluate the efficacy and safety of GLE/PIB 300/120 mg in 104 HCV-infected subjects (GT 1-6) with eGFR &lt; 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 including those on dialysis. SVR12 was achieved by 98% of the subjects, with no virologic failures, and the safety profile was comparable to that of the overall population.
L182: Demographic Factors: Demographic factors such as age, gender, race , body mass index (BMI) and compensated (Child-Pugh A) cirrhosis status were evaluated to determine if these factors have an effect on the PK of GLE and PIB. No clinically relevant effect was identified for age, race, gender, or BMI. In subjects with compensated Child-Pugh A cirrhosis (n=280), the AUC was 2.19 fold higher compared to non-cirrhotic subjects (n=1804). However, the review team fully evaluated the distribution of the AUC and determined that AUC demonstrates significant overlap between the cirrhotic and non-cirrhotic subjects; therefore, clinical recommendations pertinent to drug-drug interactions should not be different for cirrhotic subjects.
L183: Extrinsic Factors: Drug Interactions
L184: P-gp inducers: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with P-gp inducers results in a significant decrease in GLE and PIB exposures. GLE/PIB AUC decreased by 88%/87% and 67%/51% when co-administered with rifampin and carbamazepine, respectively. Moreover, following co-
L185: administration with efavirenz, GLE and PIB exposures were more than 50% lower when compared to GLE/PIB exposures observed in healthy subjects. Therefore, the co-administration of GLE/PIB and P-gp inducers, including rifampin, carbamazepine, efavirenz, and St. John's wort, should be contraindicated.
L186: HIV Protease Inhibitors: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with HIV protease inhibitors results in a significant increase in GLE and PIB exposures. Atazanavir (ATV)/ritonavir (RTV), lopinavir (LPV)/RTV, and darunavir (DRV)/RTV increased GLE AUC by 6.35-, 4.97-, and 4.38- fold respectively. ALT elevations were observed upon co-administration of GLE/PIB and ATV/RTV in healthy subjects. Based on these data, the co-administration of GLE/PIB and ATV/RTV should be contraindicated. There were no safety signals observed in the healthy volunteer studies of GLE/PIB with LPV/RTV or DRV/RTV, but due to GLE exposure increases the coadministration of GLE/PIB with these medications is not recommended. There is an ongoing trial M14-730 (EXPEDITION-2) in (B) (4) HCV/HIV-1 coinfected subjects. Formal submission of M14-730 will be requested as a post-marketing requirement.
L187: Cyclosporine: The administration of a single 100 mg cyclosporine dose increases GLE AUC by 37% and the administration of a single 400 mg cyclosporine dose increases GLE exposure by 5-fold. The exposure may be higher with multiple dosing; however, this was not evaluated in this DDI study. The co-administration of GLE/PIB with cyclosporine is not recommended due to GLE exposure increase.
L188: Ethinyl Estradiol containing medications: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with two oral contraceptive medications containing ethinyl estradiol resulted in ALT elevations in healthy subjects. There were no changes in the exposure of GLE, PIB, or the components of oral contraceptive. The co-administration of GLE/PIB with medications containing ethinyl estradiol is not recommended.
L189: Digoxin: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with digoxin increases digoxin AUC by 50%. Digoxin dose should be reduced by 50% when co-administered with GLE/PIB.
L190: Dabigatran: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with dabigatran increases dabigatran AUC by 2.38-fold. Dabigatran dose should be reduced to 75 mg BID when co-administered with GLE/PIB in subjects with creatinine clearance between 30 and 50 mL/min. The co-administration of GLE/PIB with dabigatran should be avoided in subjects with creatinine clearance &lt; 30 mL/min.
L191: HMG-CoA Reductase Inhibitors: The co-administration of GLE/PIB with statins increases atorvastatin, simvastatin, simvastatin hydroxyl acid, lovastatin, lovastatin hydroxy acid, pravastatin, and rosuvastatin by 8.27-, 2.32-, 4.48-, 1.70-, 4.10-, 2.30-, and 2.15-fold, respectively. The co-administration of GLE/PIB with simvastatin, lovastatin, and atorvastatin is not recommended. When co-administered with GLE/PIB, pravastatin dose should be reduced
L192: Clinical Review
  Larissa Stabinski, MD
  Aimee Hodowanec, MD
  NDA 209394
  Mayvret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L193: by 50% and rosuvastatin dose should not exceed 10 mg.
L194: Please see the Clinical Pharmacology review by Dr. Amal Ayyoub for further details.

L218: Dr. Lara Stabinski is the primary clinical reviewer for eight clinical trials presented in Section 8.7 [ENDURANCE-1 (M13-590), ENDURANCE-2 (M15-464), ENDURANCE-3 (M15-594), ENDURANCE-4 (M13-583), SURVEYOR-1 (M14-867), SURVEYOR-2 (M14-868), EXPEDITION-1(M14-172) and
L219: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L220: MAGELLAN-1 (M15-410)] evaluating subjects with compensated liver disease without severe renal impairment. Dr. Aimee Hodowanec is the primary clinical reviewer for the clinical trial evaluating subjects with severe disease renal disease [EXPEDITION-4 (M15-462)].
L221: The clinical efficacy review is based on the nine pivotal Phase 2 and Phase 3 trials described in Section 5.1. The clinical reviewers along with the statistical and virology reviewers collaborated extensively during the review process, and a number of analyses included in this review were performed by the statistical reviewers, Drs. Laree Tracy and Therri Usher, and the virology reviewer, Dr. Patrick Harrington. In addition, there were significant interactions with the clinical pharmacology, pharmacometrics, pharmacology/toxicology, and chemistry manufacturing and controls reviewers. Their assessments are summarized in this document in the relevant sections, but complete descriptions of their findings are available in their respective discipline reviews.
L222: This clinical review provides a complementary assessment of efficacy focused on clinically relevant genotype-specific efficacy and issues of trial design. Section 6 provides a review of the pooled Phase 2/3 data from across the registrational datasets. MAGELLAN-1, the single study including NS5A- and/or NS3/4A PI-experienced subjects, and EXPEDITION-4, including subjects with severe renal impairment, are also reviewed separately in this section. Please refer to statistics and virology reviews for further details on the nature of individual trial design, statistics and virology outcomes.
L223: Only the primary efficacy endpoint, SVR12, will be discussed in detail in this review, accompanied by a discussion regarding virologic status of subjects who did not achieve SVR12 and a discussion regarding efficacy outcomes by HCV genotype and other relevant factors. Detailed analyses of other endpoints such as SVR4, percentage of subjects with HCV RNA &lt; LLOQ while on treatment, and change from baseline in HCV RNA (log10 IU/mL) through end of treatment (EOT), will not be discussed here but are presented in statistics and virology reviews. Because the trials are ongoing, SVR24 data are not available for a significant proportion of Phase 3 subjects, and therefore are not discussed this review.
L224: The clinical safety review was primarily based on the nine aforementioned trials; data from ENDURANCE-1, ENDURANCE-2, ENDURANCE-3, ENDURANCE-4, SURVEYOR-1, SURVEYOR-2, EXPEDITION-1 and MAGELLAN-1 were pooled to include only the trial arms that contained the dose and durations to support labeling and the comparator arms from ENDURANCE-2 (placebo) and ENDURANCE-3 (daclatasvir/sofosbuvir) to form the integrated Phase 2/3 safety dataset (n=2,265). Safety data from EXPEDITION-4 was analyzed separately, and is discussed in Section 8.7.
L225: Drs. Stabinski and Hodowanec used JReview, JMP, and MAED software to conduct the safety
L226: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L227: analyses presented in this review; any analyses performed by the Applicant or other members of the FDA review team will be labeled as such. This NDA was also a part of the JumpStart Program, which confirmed the data fitness and overall safety findings of the independent review.
L228: 6 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L229: Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L230: Each of the trials presented below was conducted under a US IND application and in accordance with recognized international scientific and ethical standards, including but not limited to the International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) guideline for Good Clinical Practice (GCP) and the original principles embodied in the Declaration of Helsinki. These standards are consistent with the requirements of the US Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) Title 21, Part 312 (21CFR312), and the European Community Directive 2001/20/EC.
L231: The trial protocols, amendments and informed consent forms were reviewed and approved by independent ethics committees (IEC) or institutional review boards (IRB) before trial initiation. Investigators (or designees) were responsible for obtaining written informed consent from each individual prior to undertaking any study-related procedures. The FDA OSI inspected selected clinical sites, which were all found acceptable. A detailed discussion of the OSI audit is available in the Clinical Inspection Summary by Dr. Antoine El-Hage.
L232: Data Quality and Integrity: Sponsor's Assurance
L233: The review team considered the Applicant's methods for assuring data quality and integrity to be adequate. These methods included investigator and study center staff training on the trial protocols and study-specific procedures, study site monitoring in accordance with ICH GCP guidelines, compliance audits of investigative sites, use of electronic case report forms (eCRFs), and use of data validation specifications along with manual data review. The Applicant reviewed eCRF data to verify protocol and GCP adherence, and to verify the data against source documentation. The Applicant confirmed that missing data, selected protocol deviations and other data inconsistencies were addressed prior to database finalization. Clinical laboratory data were transferred electronically to the Applicant using defined transfer specifications.
L234: Phase 2 and 3 Pooled Efficacy Study DataSet
L235: 6.1.1. Study Design
L236: Overview and Objective
L237: The combination treatment regimen of once-daily (QD) glecaprevir (GLE, ABT-493) and pibrentasvir (PIB, ABT-530) at the dose of GLE 300 mg and PIB 120 mg (as coformulated tablets) was developed for use in treatment-naïve (TN) and treatment-experienced (TE) hepatitis C virus (HCV) genotype (GT) 1- to GT6-infected subjects with compensated liver disease (with or without cirrhosis), including subjects with chronic kidney disease (CKD) Stage 4 or 5 and subjects coinfected with human immunodeficiency virus-1 (HIV-1).
L238: Trials included in the Phase 2 and 3 Pooled Efficacy Study Dataset include ENDURANCE-1 (M13-590), ENDURANCE-2 (M15-464), ENDURANCE-3 (M15-594), ENDURANCE-4 (M13-583), SURVEYOR-1 (M14-867), SURVEYOR-2 (M14-868), EXPEDITION-1 (M14-172), MAGELLAN-1 (M15-410) and EXPEDITION-4 (M15-462). MAGELLAN-1 (M15-410) and EXPEDITION-4 (M15-462) are also discussed separately in Section 6.2 and in Section 6.3, respectively.
L239: Analysis of Trial Designs
L240: The individual Phase 2 and 3 trials employed different and complementary study designs:
L241: ● Comparison to historical control: Studies ENDURANCE-1, ENDURANCE-2, and SURVEYOR-2 Part 4 evaluated primary efficacy compared to a historical SVR12 rate for the current standard-of-care direct-acting antiviral agent (DAA) regimen at the time of study start. A –6% margin was used for noninferiority to the historical rate in all of these comparisons. Of note, the statistical team did not agree with a noninferiority comparison compared to a historical rate, rather than an active comparator. Instead, the appropriate comparison is considered to be superiority of the lower bound of the 95% confidence interval to the historical threshold. Both analyses are discussed below, where appropriate.● Duration controlled: ENDURANCE-1, ENDURANCE-3, SURVEYOR-2 (Part 3) and MAGELLAN-1 (Part 2) each implemented a duration-controlled study design evaluating either 8 weeks versus 12 weeks with a –5% margin (ENDURANCE-1), 8 weeks versus 12 weeks with a –6% margin (ENDURANCE-3), or 12 weeks versus 16 weeks without hypothesis testing (SURVEYOR-2 Part 3 and MAGELLAN-1 Part 2) of GLE/PIB.● Placebo-controlled: ENDURANCE-2 was randomized, double-blind, and placebo-controlled, and enabled characterization of the GLE/PIB safety profile.● Active-controlled: ENDURANCE-3 was randomized and compared 12 weeks of GLE/PIB versus 12 weeks of sofosbuvir (SOF) and daclatasvir (DCV) with a –6% noninferiority margin.● Open-label, single arm: Additional studies employed single-arm, open-label, assigned
L242: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L243: duration study designs among important subpopulations, including: (1) subjects with compensated (Child-Pugh Class A) cirrhosis (EXPEDITION-1 and SURVEYOR-2 Part 3), (2) subjects with CKD Stage 4 or 5 (EXPEDITION-4), and (3) HCV GT2-, GT4-, GT5-, and GT6-infected subjects without cirrhosis (ENDURANCE-4, 12-weeks of GLE/PIB in HCV GT4, GT5 or GT6; SURVEYOR-2 Part 4: 8 weeks of GLE/PIB in HCV GT2, GT4, GT5, or GT6).
L244: A single, active comparator or historical control was not used across the registrational studies, because, at the time of study conduct, there was no single regimen that was approved across all HCV genotypes. During the conduct of ENDURANCE-2, treatment assignment remained blinded to the investigator, subject, study site personnel, and the Applicant. All other Phase 2 and 3 studies were open-label.
L245: Randomization
L246: Randomization was utilized in Studies ENDURANCE-2, ENDURANCE-3, ENDURANCE-1, SURVEYOR-2, and MAGELLAN-1. ENDURANCE-4, EXPEDITION-1, and EXPEDITION-4 were single-arm studies; therefore, no randomization was performed. SURVEYOR-1 also was not randomized.
L247: Inclusion/Exclusion Criteria
L248: Inclusion and exclusion criteria to define study populations were harmonized across the majority of registrational studies, and are described below.
L249: Key Inclusion Criteria
L250: Male or female, at least 18 years of age at time of screening
L251: • Subject had positive anti-HCV antibody and plasma HCV RNA viral load \geq 1,000 IU/mL at screening• Chronic HCV infection defined as 1 of the following:○ Positive for anti-HCV antibody or HCV RNA at least 6 months before screening; or○ A liver biopsy consistent with chronic HCV infection; or○ Abnormal alanine aminotransferase levels for at least 6 months before screening• BMI is \geq 18 kg/m2 at the time of screening• Voluntarily signed and dated an informed consent form, approved by an institutional Review Board/Independent Ethics Committee prior to the initiation of any screening or study specific procedures• Able to understand and adhere to the study visit schedule and all other protocol requirements
L252: Key Exclusion Criteria

L259: Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L260: • History of severe, life-threatening or other significant sensitivity to excipients of the study drug• Positive test result at screening for hepatitis B surface antigen (all studies) or anti-HIV-1 antibody (except ENDURANCE-1; M13-590)• Females who are pregnant or intending to become pregnant, or breastfeeding, and males with a female partner who was pregnant or is intending to become pregnant during the course of the study• HCV genotyping performed during screening indicating coinfection with more than 1 HCV genotype• Any cause of liver disease other than chronic HCV infection• Consideration by the investigator, for any reason, that the subject was an unsuitable candidate to receive GLE,PIB, or GLE/PIB• Child-Pugh B or C or history of liver decompensation.
L261: Screening laboratory inclusion criteria were similar across all 9 studies, except for small differences in the Phase 2 supportive studies (for the study/parts in cirrhotic subjects or in CKD Stage 4 or 5 subjects, as appropriate). The following laboratory parameters must have been fulfilled at screening in the majority of these studies:
L262: • Alanine aminotransferase and aspartate aminotransferase \leq 10 \times the upper limit of normal (ULN);• Direct bilirubin \leq ULN (for subjects without cirrhosis) or a total bilirubin \leq 3 \times ULN (for subjects with cirrhosis);• Platelets \geq 60,000/\mu\text{L} (\geq 40,000/\mu\text{L} in EXPEDITION-4; M15-462) and \geq 90,000/\mu\text{L} in subjects with and without cirrhosis, respectively;• Hemoglobin A1c indicating no uncontrolled diabetes (e.g., \leq 8 or \leq 8.5\%); Hemoglobin \geq the lower limit of normal;• Albumin \geq 2.8 g/dL or \geq lower limit of normal in subjects with or without cirrhosis, respectively;• International normalized ratio \leq 2.3 or \leq 1.5 in subjects with or without cirrhosis, respectively, unless subject had known hemophilia or was stable on an anticoagulant regimen affecting international normalized ratio.
L263: There were no upper limits for baseline body mass index (BMI) or age in the registrational studies. Subjects were included regardless of any positive toxicology screen result or history of bleeding disorders. The main differences between studies were related to HCV genotype, the presence or absence of severe renal impairment, and the presence or absence of compensated cirrhosis (Child-Pugh A).
L264: Genotype and Treatment Experience
L265: As shown in Section 5.1, HCV GT1- – GT6-infected subjects who were either TN or TE, including subjects with prior failure to interferon (IFN) or pegIFN \pm RBV, SOF and RBV (\pm pegIFN) were enrolled in ENDURANCE-1, ENDURANCE-2, SURVEYOR-2 Parts 3 and 4, ENDURANCE-4, ENDURANCE-2 and EXPEDITION-1. Genotype 3-infected subjects who were TN were enrolled in ENDURANCE-3. Subjects with experience to nonstructural viral protein 3/4A (NS3/4A) protease inhibitors (PI) and/or a nonstructural viral protein 5A (NS5A) inhibitors were enrolled in MAGELLAN-1. Genotype 1 HCV/HIV-1 coinfecting subjects were included in ENDURANCE-1. Subjects with a screening creatinine clearance \geq 50 mL/min were allowed into the majority of the registrational studies, while subjects with CKD Stage 4 or 5 were evaluated in a dedicated study (EXPEDITION-4).
L266: Cirrhosis Status
L267: Subjects with cirrhosis were allowed in a number of the registrational studies (EXPEDITION-1, Study SURVEYOR-2 Part 3, MAGELLAN-1 Part 2, and EXPEDITION-4). The following definition for documenting the presence or absence of cirrhosis was employed.
L268: An absence of cirrhosis was defined by documented evidence of:
L269: • A liver biopsy within 24 months prior to or during screening, demonstrating the absence of cirrhosis, e.g., a METAVIR, Batts-Ludwig, Knodell, IASL, Scheuer, or Laennec fibrosis score of \leq 3, Ishak fibrosis score of \leq 4; or• A FibroScan® score of &lt; 12.5 kPa within 6 months of screening or during the screening period; or• Subjects with an indeterminate FibroScan® score (12.5 \leq \text{score} &lt; 14.6) were to have a qualifying liver biopsy• A screening FibroTest score of \leq 0.48 and aspartate aminotransferase to platelet ratio index (APRI) &lt; 1.• Subjects with an indeterminate FibroTest (0.48 &lt; \text{result} &lt; 0.75), or conflicting FibroTest and APRI results (e.g., FibroTest \leq 0.48, but APRI \geq 1) were to have a qualifying FibroScan® or qualifying liver biopsy
L270: The presence of cirrhosis was defined by documented evidence of:
L271: • Histologic diagnosis of cirrhosis on a liver biopsy e.g., a METAVIR, Batts-Ludwig, Knodell, IASL, Scheuer, or Laennec fibrosis score of &gt; 3 or Ishak fibrosis score of &gt; 4; or FibroScan® score \geq 14.6 kPa;○ Subjects with an indeterminate FibroScan® score (12.5 \leq \text{score} &lt; 14.6) were to have a qualifying liver biopsy; or• A screening FibroTest score of \geq 0.75 and APRI &gt; 2;○ Subjects with an indeterminate FibroTest (0.48 &lt; \text{result} &lt; 0.75) or discordant FibroTest and APRI results (e.g., FibroTest \geq 0.75, but APRI \leq 2) were to have a qualifying
L272: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L273: FibroScan® or liver biopsy
L274: Study Endpoints
L275: The primary efficacy endpoint across all trials was SVR12, defined as HCV RNA &lt; lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ) 12 weeks after discontinuation of the study drug.
L276: Secondary efficacy endpoints included the number and percentage of subjects with on-treatment virologic failure and with relapse before or during the SVR12 window (Relapse12). HCV RNA levels in plasma were measured using the Roche COBAS® AmpliPrep/COBAS® Taqman® HCV Test, v2.0, with a LLOQ of 15 IU/mL and a lower limit of detection of 15 IU/mL. Virologic relapse was defined as confirmed HCV RNA ≥ LLOQ between end of treatment and 12 weeks after the last dose of study drug among subjects who completed treatment as planned with HCV RNA &lt; LLOQ at the end of treatment.
L277: Virologic failure was defined as confirmed increase of &gt; 1 log10 IU/mL above nadir during treatment, confirmed HCV RNA ≥ 100 after HCV RNA &lt; LLOQ during treatment, or HCV RNA ≥ LLOQ at the end of treatment with at least 6 weeks of treatment.
L278: The primary efficacy analysis was performed on the intent-to-treat (ITT) population, which included all subjects who received at least one dose of study medication. Please refer above to Section 5.1, the trial design descriptions in this section and to Drs. Tracy and Usher’s statistics review for complete details.
L279: 6.1.2. Study Results
L280: Patient Disposition
L281: A total of 2,376 subjects were randomized or enrolled, of which 2,369 subjects received at least 1 dose of study drug and were included in the Phase 2 and 3 Analysis Set. One hundred and four of these subjects had severe renal impairment were enrolled in EXPEDITION-4. Overall, the percentage of subjects who prematurely discontinued study drug for any reason was low (37 [1.6%] of subjects). The most common primary reason for premature discontinuation of study drug was due to adverse events (11 [0.5%] subjects) followed by virologic failure (8 [0.3%] subjects), and ≤0.2% of subjects discontinued for reasons of withdrawn consent, loss to follow-up or other reasons.

L318: Efficacy by Genotype
L319: The efficacy discussion focuses on a genotype-based analysis and is meant to complement the thorough trial by trial statistical review and the detailed virology reviews from a clinical perspective. Discussion of each genotype, by treatment experience, cirrhosis status and treatment duration culminates in a reviewer comment that includes the final clinical assessment of the reviewer and the overall review team regarding the indication by genotype. The reviewer concurred with the review team on all below issues regarding efficacy. The efficacy by genotype discussion excludes the NS3/4A PI- and/or NS5A Inhibitor- DAA-experienced population, which is covered separately in Section 6.2 in the review of MAGELLAN-1. An additional, separate discussion of efficacy for patients with severe renal impairment is also presented in Section 6.3 in the review of EXPEDITION-4, although data from EXPEDITION-4 are also included in the below genotype discussion, as efficacy did not differ in this sub-population.
L320: GENOTYPE 1
L321: Overall Genotype 1(GT1) Data: As shown in Table 8, there were a total of 889 HCV GT1, NS3/4A PI- and NS5A Inhibitor-Naïve subjects in the Phase 2/3 dataset treated for 8 or 12 weeks duration. Of these, 101 (11.4%) were subjects with compensated cirrhosis and 561 (63.1%) were treatment naïve. Subjects with treatment experience either had interferon (INF) or pegylated interferon, and ribavirin (P/R) experience or experience with sofosbuvir and ribavirin or P/R (TE-PRS). Thirty-three GT-1 subjects were co-infected with HIV, including 18 subjects in the GLE/PIB 12 week arm and 15 in the 8 week arm.
L322: In total, across all 889 GT1 subjects treated for 8 or 12 weeks duration, there were two virologic failures, both in ENDURANCE-1. One non-cirrhotic subject who received 8 weeks duration
L323: experienced on-treatment virologic failure, and one cirrhotic subject who received 12 weeks duration experienced relapse; neither subject was co-infected with HIV. In addition, six subjects did not achieve SVR12 for non-virologic reasons including discontinuation of treatment (3) or missing SVR12 values (3). One of these 6 subjects was treatment naïve and non-cirrhotic and received the 12 week treatment duration, which was not proposed for labeling.
L324: Statistical Considerations of GT1 Data: HCV GT1, TN and TE-PRS subjects were enrolled in ENDURANCE-1, ENDURANCE-2, SURVEYOR-1, SURVEYOR-2, EXPEDITION-1 and EXPEDITION-4. Each trial met its statistical thresholds for GT1 analysis, and all trials were adequately conducted. Overall, GT1 SVR12 data by trial, treatment experience, cirrhosis status and treatment duration are shown in the below table.

L330: Treatment-Naïve or PRS- Treatment Experienced Without Cirrhosis for 8 weeks or 12 weeks
L331: ENDURANCE-1 enrolled the largest proportion of TN and TE-PRS GT1 non-cirrhotic subjects [(703/889), 79.1%]. ENDURANCE-1 was a Phase 3, randomized, open-label, parallel-group, multicenter study, in TN and TE-PRS GT1 HCV or HCV/HIV-1 co-infected adult subjects, without cirrhosis, to evaluate the safety and efficacy of 8- and 12-weeks of GLE/PIB. Randomization to either an 8- or 12-week regimen was performed in a 1:1 ratio, stratified by screening viral load (&lt; or ≥ 6 million IU/mL) and HCV GT1 subtype (1a or 1b). Safety assessments were planned out to 24 weeks post-end of treatment.
L332: The primary trial objective was to demonstrate non-inferiority of the 12-week GLE/PIB regimen to a historical control of 97% SVR12 using a 6% NI margin. The justification for the 97% pre-specified clinical threshold was based on data from a Phase 3 trial among GT1 HCV DAA-naïve subjects who were non-cirrhotic receiving ombitasvir/paritaprevir/ritonavir+dasabuvir+/-RBV in addition to findings from previous trials of SOF/LDV (ION-1, ION-2, and ION-3), which demonstrated a high 97% SVR12. However, Phase 3 trials leading to this estimate included a SOC, at that time, of an IFN-based regimen. The SVR in TN subjects who received an IFN-based regimen was approximately 78%. Therefore, the difference, or M1, was 19% (i.e. 97%-78%). Thus, with a 6% margin, the lower bound that must be exceeded in ENDURANCE-1 was 91%.
L333: The SVR12 in 8 week and 12 week GLE/PIB groups was 99.1% 95%CI: (97.5, 99.7) and 99.7% 95% CI: (98.4, 100), respectively in the ITT population of ENDURANCE-1. One subject in the GLE/PIB 8 week arm was an on-treatment failure, two subjects were missing the SVR12 assessment and one subject prematurely discontinued treatment on study Day 2. The lower bound around the SVR12 in the GLE/PIB 12 week regimen was 98.3% well above the pre-specified 91% target. The difference between treatment groups was -0.6% (8-12 weeks) with a lower bound of -2% above the -5% pre-specified NI margin. Therefore, the data supported an 8 week treatment duration in GT1 treatment-naïve or treatment-experienced-PRS subjects without cirrhosis.
L334: However, the FDA statistical review team did not agree with the Applicant’s non-inferiority approach, without inclusion of an active comparator regimen in the trial. Therefore, their analysis was one of superiority, evaluating the lower bound of the 95%CI compared to the
L335: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L336: clinically acceptable threshold of 91%. As discussed above, both the 8 and 12 week arms demonstrated superiority, with a lower bound of the 95%CI of 97.5% and 98.4% for the 8 and 12 week arms, respectively.
L337: Of note, additional supportive data were evaluated from SURVEYOR-1 (n=5) and SURVEYOR-2 (n=2), both evaluating GLE/PIB for 8 weeks duration and ENDURANCE-2 (n=3), evaluating 12 weeks duration supporting the treatment of TE-PRS subjects without cirrhosis (see table above). All 10 subjects achieved SVR.
L338: Treatment-Naïve and Treatment Experienced-PRS With Compensated Cirrhosis for 12 weeks
L339: Data to support treatment-naïve subjects with compensated cirrhosis for 12 weeks were derived from EXPEDITION-1 and EXPEDITION-4. Both trials were non-randomized, single arm, non-comparative trials; and therefore statistical results were described via a two-sided 95% CI calculated using the Wilson method, due to the high rate of SVR12 (see table above and statistical review for details). Most [(87/101), 86.1%] of the GT1 subjects with compensated cirrhosis were enrolled in EXPEDITION-1.
L340: In treatment-naïve subjects with cirrhosis from EXPEDITION-1 and EXPEDITION-4, the SVR12 rates were 100% (66/66) with a 95%CI (94.5, 100), and 81.8% (9/11) with a 95%CI (52.3, 94.9), respectively. Both subjects who did not achieve SVR12 had non-virologic failure. The SVR12 rate was 95.8% (23/24) with a 95%CI of (79.8, 99.3) in treatment-experienced-PRS subjects with cirrhosis from EXPEDITION-1; the one subject who did not achieve SVR12 had virologic relapse.
L341: Subgroup analyses were not conducted given the very high SVR 12 in this population. There were no major additional statistics considerations. Please see the statistics review for further details.
L342: Clinical Virology Considerations:
L343: Among noncirrhotic HCV GT1 infected subjects without cirrhosis treated with GLE/PIB for 8 weeks, 1/387 (0.3%) subjects experienced virologic failure. The single virologic failure subject experienced on-treatment failure and not relapse. A longer treatment duration would not have influenced treatment outcome in this single subject with on-treatment virologic failure. Therefore, GLE/PIB for 8 weeks is well supported for noncirrhotic, HCV GT1 infected patients. Among cirrhotic HCV GT1 infected subjects treated with GLE/PIB for 12 weeks, 1/101 (1%) experienced virologic failure (relapse).
L344: The near 100% efficacy of GLE/PIB indicates that natural baseline HCV resistance-associated polymorphisms did not significantly influence treatment efficacy for HCV GT1 infected subjects. Considering 9 resistance-associated amino acid positions in NS5A, amino acid polymorphisms
L345: Clinical ReviewLarissa Stabinski, MDAimee Hodowanec, MDNDA 209394Mavyret® (glecaprevir and pibrentasvir)
L346: were detected at the \geq 15\% nucleotide sequence level in 21% (78/380) and 32% (147/461) of GT1a- and GT1b-infected subjects, respectively, who had no previous exposure to NS3 protease and NS5A inhibitors. Both virologic failure subjects had GT1a virus with two resistance-associated polymorphisms, although only one polymorphism was detected in one subject at the \geq 15\% level. Ten subjects with HCV GT1a infection had \geq 2 NS5A resistance-associated polymorphisms detected at the \geq 15\% level, and all 10 achieved SVR12. Both virologic failure subjects also had \geq 2 treatment-emergent/enriched NS5A resistance-associated substitutions (RASs) detected at the time of virologic failure, and one subject had a treatment-emergent NS3 A156V substitution. Please see the virology review for more details.
L347: Additional Clinical Data/ Considerations: Additional details regarding the subjects with on treatment failure and relapse are briefly described below. Although data were limited given that there was only one on treatment failure and one virologic relapse, there was no indication that age, sex or race played a role in these virologic failures.
L348: Subject 106104 is a 60 year old non-cirrhotic white male with a previous treatment history of P/R experienced virologic break-through on day 29 and subsequently withdrew from the study.
L349: Subject 203501 is a 62 year old white female with cirrhosis and a baseline CTP 5 and MELD 7 and a previous history of treatment with P/R who failed to achieve SVR12.
L350: Reviewer Comment: Given the high SVR rates and robust data for GT1 TN and TE-PRS in this application, the review team supports the indication of 8 weeks of therapy for subjects without cirrhosis and 12 weeks of therapy for subjects with compensated cirrhosis (CTP A liver disease) in this population. From a general safety standpoint, this indication also minimizes the exposure time to GLE/PIB, decreasing the possibility of any associated adverse events and possibility for drug-drug interactions without sacrificing efficacy.
L351: Although the numbers of HIV-co-infected subjects were small (n=33), there were no virologic failures in this subpopulation. Further, as HIV status is not expected to have a direct effect on efficacy, recommendations for treatment of HIV-co-infected subjects should mirror the overall treatment recommendations by genotype and treatment experience. This recommendation is bolstered by additional summary data provided by the Applicant at midcycle showing SVR12 results of 100% from an additional 21 HCV GT3 infected subjects with HIV-1 coinfection, who received GLE/PIB for 8 weeks in an ongoing trial, EXPEDITION-2 (M14-730).
L352: GENOTYPE 2

L354: compensated cirrhosis and 369 (79.2%) were treatment-naïve. Subjects with treatment experience either had INF or P/R experience or experience with SOF and RBV or P/R. Two non-cirrhotic subjects experienced virologic failures; both subjects experienced relapse. Four subjects did not achieve SVR12 for non-virologic reasons including missing SVR12 data (1) and discontinuation of treatment (3).

Each trial met its statistical thresholds for GT2 analysis and the trials were adequately conducted.
